# Executive Summary

This report presents findings of a rapid gender analysis study commissioned by the National Environmental Management Authority (NEMA) Kenya through partnerships with the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) and the World Bank. The study aims to understand the impacts of COVID-19 on gender equality, livelihoods, and environmental conservation in Kenya. Specifically, the gender analysis seeks to identify and proper mechanisms to enhance women's participation in decision-making processes at various levels. The report also highlights the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on the gender dynamics that could exacerbate existing or generate new forms of disparities in the way women and men experience environmental changes.

The gender analysis was cross-sectional using qualitative methods. Emphasis was placed on understanding the impacts of COVID-19 on livelihoods, health, and well-being through observation and workshops in Nyanza, Kamba, Makueni, and North-Eastern counties. The study also examined the impact of COVID-19 on gender equality and women's empowerment by analyzing the participation, balance of power and decision-making, rights and status, and time and space constraints. The analysis included an assessment of the impacts of COVID-19 on livelihoods through intensive desk reviews. The main findings of the study are:

- **Economic Impacts**: Loss of income, employment, and skills.
- **Health Impacts**: Increased cases of gender-based violence (GBV).
- **Environmental Impacts**: Increased use of firewood and charcoal, leading to deforestation.

The outcomes indicate that understanding the impacts of COVID-19 on gender issues is critical for developing policies to enhance women's participation in environmental conservation. Furthermore, the study found a need to provide data to inform the development of policies that have enhanced gender equality and women's empowerment. The study also highlights the need for more comprehensive data collection and analysis to generate evidence-based policy recommendations.

**Chapter Three: Methodology** - This part of the report summarises the research process followed in undertaking the gender analysis.

**Chapter Four: Findings** - This section of the reports scrutinizes and analyses feedback from the fieldwork undertaken during the gender analysis reviews.

**Chapter Five: Summary and Recommendations** - This part of the report condenses the findings into a summary and provides recommendations based on them.

**Annex** - This section of the report presents an action plan based on the findings and recommendations.

## 1. Country Context

Kenya underwent resurveying in September 2014 that confirmed on the country the lower middle-income status with the fourth largest economy in sub-Saharan Africa after Nigeria, South Africa and Egypt. Despite women's opposition starting eight years ago at 8.6%, 639,000 women were added to the national population of 45.07 million. The GDP is at KES 2.1 trillion. The country has a youthful population with 73% under 35 years and an average age of 20 years. The country has a life expectancy of 62 years, including long-lasting issues of diseases. This demographic group has exerted pressure on the country's resources for sustenance, highlighting the critical and persistent disparity between environmental concerns and community needs.

According to the Kenya National Bureau of Statistics (KNBS, 2019), census results show a rise in an increment of population from 38.6 million in 2009 to 47.548 million in 2019. The total national population is currently at 47.548 million. Household size has reduced from 4.3m in 2009 to 3.9m in 2019. It is a confirmation that women are more than men as per the trend. # Kenya

Terriorial area for Kenya is **582,644** km², including some areas of 1230km² and land area of 57,416 km². The major part of the isolated water effects are covered by a portion of the lake. The country has a population of about **49 million**. The average annual rainfall is **885** mm and the land area is classified as an arid/semi-arid land (ASAL). The amount of land under irrigation is about 300,000 hectares, which is used for agricultural purposes.

The major economic base for Kenya is tourism. The Kenya Tourism Board is the key agency in charge of promoting and regulating the tourism industry. The tourism sector contributes significantly to the economy, accounting for **16%** of the total exports and providing jobs to over **1 million** people. The tourism sector has been a major source of foreign exchange earnings and a significant contributor to the national economy.

## 1.2 Water Resources in Kenya

The main water resources in Kenya are surface water, commonly Lake Naivasha North Catchment Area (VNICA), Lake Victoria South Catchment Area (LVSCA), Rift Valley Catchment Area (RVCA) and Tana River Catchment Area (TRCA). According to the Kenya Water Resources Authority (WRA), the amount of water available in 2019 was **5,648** million cubic meters (MCM). The amount of water used is estimated at **3,784** MCM/year. This includes water for domestic use, agriculture and industry.

### 1.3 Gender and Equity Highlights for Kenya

Equity in water resources management is a human rights issue and development imperative. Through high-level instruments, policy frameworks, and institutional mechanisms, the Government of Kenya has made significant strides towards addressing inequality and discrimination as prerequisites for ensuring a progressive and inclusive society. The Constitution of Kenya 2010 recognizes the right to clean and safe water, which is fundamental to human dignity, equality, and sustainable development. The Constitution also provides that every person has the right to a clean and healthy environment.

Gender equity in water resources management is a key priority for the Government of Kenya. Gender government mechanisms are therefore designed to ensure that gender issues are mainstreamed into all policies, programs, and projects. The National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC) was established by the Constitution. Development government mechanisms are therefore designed to ensure that gender issues are mainstreamed into all policies, programs, and projects.

The mandate of institutions, like the National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC), is to promote and protect gender equality and equity in development and implementation. Yet, despite the robust normative frameworks equally beneficial to both men and women, there remain significant gaps in the realization of these rights.

## 1.4 Gender and Water Resource Interactions

Water is a critical factor in producing the outputs needed to households for both men and women. Access to water underlies women's and men's participation in reproductive (women) and productive (men) activities. The Kenya Water Supply and Sanitation Program (KWSP) reported that despite 68% of the urban population in Kenya has access to improved water sources, only 30% of rural households have access to safe drinking water. This indicates a severe constraint to women's and girls' ability to maintain their health and hygiene needs. The KWSP also found that 72% of daily household water-related tasks were done by women, and girls spend more time fetching water than boys. Women and girls are more likely to be exposed to disease from other sources if the water is unsafe or contaminated. This gender disparity in water resource management affects women's ability to participate fully in economic activities, education, and healthcare. The KWSP also found that women were more likely to be involved in the use, enjoyment, and evolution of ecosystem services (e.g., water purification) than men.

## 1.5 Global Climate Fund Programmes

The GCF contains or supports health centers for gender-responsive country programs and activities that benefit marginalized groups. The GCF also funds projects that address gender equality and climate change issues. These include projects that promote women's empowerment, gender equality, and the rights of indigenous peoples. The GCF also funds projects that support women's and girls' resilience to climate change and their participation in decision-making processes related to climate change. The GCF also supports projects that help the same groups adapt to climate change.

The gender analysis was cross-sectional, using mostly qualitative methods. English was the main language of communication in all the focus groups and key informant interviews. Observations and workshops in the focus counties. The consultants used purposive sampling to select respondents from the community, local government officials, and members of the community. Focus group discussions (FGD) were conducted with a sample size of 20-30 people per county.

Focus group discussions (FGD) were used to obtain views of groups of stakeholders in all counties. The focus groups were purposively selected to give views on the needs of water resource users and the community. The focus groups were conducted with a sample size of 20-30 people per county.

# 3.1 The Aire Catchment Area

ACA is located in the southern part of the county as shown in Figure (1.3) and borders on the True Catchment Area, the north, the Ingleby Green, the east, Tansley in the south, and the Derwent Valley to the west. The area lies between 53°04'N and 53°08'N latitude and 1°29'W and 1°36'W longitude, but is not defined by these limits. ACA lies in the northern edge of the Peak District National Park (PDNP) and covers an area of approximately 374 km². The population of ACA in 2001 was estimated at 9,785 million, or 23.4% of the total population of Derbyshire.

The topography of ACA varies from the highland in the Aberdale Range at around 2,600 m to the lowlands in the south of the catchment area at approximately 150 m above sea level. The highest point is 384 m and the lowest point is 97 m. The catchment area is drained by the River Aire which flows from the north-west to the south-east. The drainage area of the Aire River is 2,760 km² or 44% of Peak Catchment Area. The main tributaries are the River Derwent and the River Rother which flow into the River Aire at Matlock Bath and Cromford respectively. The catchment area also includes the River Wye which flows from the southern edge of the catchment area to the total drainage area is 19,490 km².

ACA is classified as a semi-natural catchment area using the optimum area of the Aire River which is approximately 374 km². The catchment area is surrounded by the Peak District National Park and the Derwent Valley Mills World Heritage Site. The population density in the catchment area, estimated at 5.84 BP/km² for 2010 for ACA and the surrounding areas.

Source: Source: Ministry of Environment, Water and Natural Resources, 2013.

# Enhancing the resilience of communities and ecosystems to the Athabasca River Catchment Area

The National Environmental Management Authority (NEMA), Kenya’s nationally accredited environmental regulator, has received a $1 million grant from the Global Environment Facility (GEF) through the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). The funds will be used to enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems in the Athabasca River Catchment Area (ARCA).

NEMA submitted a draft proposal of the project to the GEF Secretariat in 2016 to secure funding. The project aims to address the environmental challenges facing the ARCA, including climate change impacts on water security, biodiversity loss, and ecosystem degradation. The well-being of the vulnerable populations within the Athabasca River Catchment Area (ARCA) is also a key focus.

The ARCA comprises a semi-arid land and suffers from frequent drought and flooding events. These challenges have been exacerbated by human activities such as deforestation and unsustainable farming practices. During the long dry spells, there has been an increase in human-wildlife conflict due to the search for water and pasture. The floods experienced in March, April, and May 2018 led to the destruction of property and infrastructure, displacement of people, and loss of lives.

The project is designed to address environmental and water-related challenges of the ARCA through sustainable land management practices that promote ecosystem resilience and sustainable livelihoods. Specifically, the greater ARCA’s ecological integrity will be identified and protected from further degradation. The project aims to enhance the adaptive capacity of communities in the area by promoting sustainable land use, water resource management, and climate change mitigation strategies. The project will also support the integration of climate-smart practices into agricultural systems and promote the adoption of renewable energy sources.

The project is expected to contribute to the achievement of the following outcomes:

- Enhanced resilience of ecosystems and communities to climate change.
- Improved water security for both people and wildlife in the ARCA.
- Increased adoption of sustainable land management practices by local communities.
- Strengthened institutional capacity to manage and protect the ARCA’s natural resources.

The project will be implemented over a period of five years, with an expected completion date of December 2025. The total budget for the project is $1 million, with the GEF providing $800,000 and the Government of Kenya contributing the remaining $200,000.

# Karen Red Cross Report

The Red Cross Society of Kenya (RCSEK) has released a report on the impact of climate change on the Athabasca River Catchment Area. The report highlights the need for urgent action to address the environmental challenges facing the region.

# Methodology

## Gender Analysis Approach, Framework and Domains

The analysis adopted a gender and development approach whose main focus is the unequal power relations between men and women that affect their access to resources and opportunities for participation in decision-making processes and projects tasks is empowered by the Women's Empowerment in Agriculture Index (WEAI). The WEAI framework was useful in elevating the analysis to respond to the global mandate of promoting gender equality and women empowerment. The index measures women's access to resources, knowledge, beliefs, and perceptions; practices and decision-making; time use; and empowerment within households over time and space.

## Study Design

The study design adopted a mixed-methods approach using both qualitative and quantitative methods. Emphasis was placed on making the process participatory through focus group discussions, key informant interviews, semi-structured and unstructured interviews. Household surveys were conducted to collect data from women, men, and youth regarding their access to resources, services delivery efficient and policy makers in the water and environment management sectors, and gender mainstreaming activities.

# 3.4 Organization of Gender Analysis and Quality Assurance

Workshops in Tanzania, Rwanda, Malawi and Nigeria were used as entry points for data collection and identification of sites for prompt interventions. The use included by participants was guided by a gender analysis toolkit developed by Oxfam GB. The toolkit was designed to ensure that the research issues considered comprehensive terms on the gender analysis framework and the context of the study area. The toolkit was used to identify key informants, focus group discussions and other data collection methods were transcribed and subjected to qualitative analysis strategies and techniques. The toolkit was also used to develop a gender analysis matrix for the identification of common patterns and trends arising from the narratives. Content analysis of the narratives was conducted using the gender analysis framework.

# 3.5 Compliance with Research Ethics

Participation in the gender analysis was voluntary and based on informed consent. It included ethical considerations such as confidentiality, privacy and anonymity. In the greater analysis, benefits of their participation both directly and indirectly and fully understood the purpose of the research. The participants were assured that their participation would not be used to harm them or others. The study team ensured the research team and the participants is well aware among the study participants.

# 3.6 Research Constraints and Limitations

The major constraint was the limited time available for data collection on gender issues in the country. The limited time available for data collection was due to the need to meet the deadline of the project. The limited time also affected the quality of the data collected which may not be generalized across other different contexts.

# 3.7 Structure of the Gender Analysis Report

## Chapter One: Introduction
This section contains an overview of the development progress in Kenya and the gender analysis framework used for the study.

## Chapter Two: Gender Analysis
The section of this report briefly outlined the gender analysis framework used to guide the research.

## Chapter Three: Methodology
This part of the report summarizes the research process including the data collection methods, sampling techniques and data analysis procedures.

## Chapter Four: Findings
This part of the reports includes and analyzes findings relevant through various sections and understanding the gender analysis, including literature review and recommendations.

## Chapter Five: Summary and Recommendations
This part of the report concludes the study and provides an action plan based on the findings and recommendations.

# 4. Findings

## 4.1 Kenya Gender Profile

### 4.1.1 Population

Physical growth in Kenya has been characterized by high fertility rates and high population growth rates. During the last five decades, the country's total population grew from 8.0 million in 1962 to an estimated 37.5 million in 2009. The annual population growth rate was 4.1% between 1962 and 1979, 3.2% between 1979 and 1989, and 2.5% between 1989 and 2009. The 2009 census estimated the population at approximately 40 million people, with an annual growth rate of 2.6%. A total fertility rate of 3.7 children per woman was recorded in the Kenya Demographic and Health Survey (KDHS) 2008-09.

#### Table 8: Rural and Urban Population Sizes

| Area       | Rural Population (000s) | Urban Population (000s) | Total Population (000s) |
|------------|--------------------------|--------------------------|------------------------|
| Buru      | 1,593.467                | 1,208.075                | 28,422.72              |
| Uasin Gishu | 6,578.811               | 6,580.364                | 13,467.77              |

#### Source: Population and Housing Census 2009

### 4.1.2 Health

Physical mortality rates in Kenya are estimated at 442 maternal deaths per 100,000 live births, with an infant mortality rate of 53 deaths per 1,000 live births. The latest Kenya AIDS Indicator Survey (KAIS) 2007 reported a prevalence rate of 6.7% among adults aged 15-49 years, with higher rates in urban areas at 8.5%. The KAIS report indicates that 21% of new HIV infections occur among young people aged 15-24 years.

The health sector in Kenya has been documented as having substantial and humanitarian emergencies. The National Crime Research Centre (NCRC) reported that the country had a total of 3,087 homicides in 2009, with an increase from 2,697 in 2008. The NCRC also reported that the number of people living with HIV/AIDS increased by 15% between 2007 and 2009.

#### References

- [Source 1](https://www.kdnr.go.ke/)
- [Source 2](https://www.ncrck.org/)


---
**Page 124**
---

The 1998 KDH reported 38 % of women in Kenya having been circumcised, which declined to 27% in 2003, 27% in 2008-09, and 21% in 2014. This can be attributed to innovations on the ground aimed at reducing the prevalence of female genital mutilation (FGM) and changing social norms regarding FGM. The government has also been involved in the enforcement of the Prohibition against Female Genital Mutilation Act (2011).

### 4.1.4 Household Headship

Most households in Kenya are male-headed (57.6% male and 32.4% female) with an average household size of 4.8 persons. The proportion of female-headed households is highest among the 25-49 age groups, with 40.1% of such households headed by women compared to 18.4% in the older age group. Household headship is highest in the coastal region and lowest in the Rift Valley. Nairobi has the highest proportion of female-headed households (37.5%), followed by Nyanza (36.9%) and Western (32.0%). The Central region has the lowest proportion at 18.4%.

![Figure 1: Household Headship](image_url)

Nairobi leads in the male household headship and contrary patterns in female heads. It is followed by Kakamega, Nyanza than Mombasa. However, Machakos was found to have the highest proportion of female-headed households (40.1%) compared to other regions. Nairobi has the lowest proportion of female-headed households at 37.5%.

## 4.1.4 National Priorities

The government has put in place a number of policies aimed at improving the livelihoods of Kenyans. One such policy is the provision of free primary education. This has led to an increase in enrollment rates and improved access to basic education for children from poor families. The government also provides free secondary education for needy students as well as bursaries and scholarships for those who qualify.

### 4.2 Access to Resources

#### 4.2.1 Access to Basic Education for Men and Women

Access to both basic and tertiary education in Kenya has grown exponentially over the last 15 years. The Gross Enrollment Ratio (GER) for primary education is currently at 97.3% while that of secondary education stands at 68.4%. GER is an indicator used by UNESCO to measure the proportion of students enrolled in a particular level of education relative to the population of the same age group.

Notably, the gross attendance ratio (GAR) for pre-primary school is 94.4% (95.6% for males and 93.2% for females). For primary school, it rises to 107.8% (109.0% for males and 106.6% for females). The GAR for secondary school is 84.2% (87.2% for males and 85.2% for females).

*The Net Attendance Ratio (NAR) is defined as the total number of females in the official school-age group divided by the total number of males in the same age group. Source: Ministry of Education, Science and Technology (MEST), Basic Education Statistical Booklet 2014.*

## 4.2 Main Sources of Livelihood and Income for Women and Men

### Key Findings

- Women engage more at the main sources of livelihood and income generation.
- The major productive resources and social amenities that enrich livelihoods in Nyandarua, Kamba, and Machakos counties exist, households, schools, roads, and buildings. According to the Kenya National Bureau of Statistics (KNBS), 2018, women are more engaged in agricultural activities than men.
- On the other hand, men in the three counties recognized as a source of income generation are more involved in livestock farming compared to women. The majority of women (67%) are engaged in agriculture and livestock farming, while only 35% of men are engaged in these activities. Women also play an important role in household chores and income generation.
- The main sources of livelihood and income for women and men are similar but differ in the proportion of engagement.

### 4.3.3 Land as a Production Resource

Land is a productive resource that was consistently highly ranked by men and community leaders in both Kambu and Machakos. Women also ranked land high but not as high as men did for similar reasons: when sold it was a factor of production. Women in Kambu reported the land is usually owned by men, and they have no control over its use. They described that both men and women have rights to land but women's control is limited and mainly during their husband’s absence (when he sold and rented out land). Land was owned by men; customary laws were also stated by men; the land was controlled by men who held title deeds to the land. Women had no control over the use of the land, and they were not allowed to sell it without permission from their husbands. They reported that women and children had access to land only when men were away (for example, during harvest time). Women’s control over land was limited by customary laws and by the fact that they did not have title deeds to the land. Men controlled the use of the land and the basis of production in the Constitution of Kenya (2010) and the Land Act 2012.

#### Table 10: Land Related Constraints and Coping Mechanisms

| County | Gender | Constraints | Coping Mechanisms |
|--------|---------|--------------|-------------------|
| Kambu  | Women   | Small size of land owned for production | Women are allowed to use the land and cultivate crops for subsistence living. |
|        | Men     | Small size of land owned for children’s school fees and household needs | Women grow crops for subsistence living. |
| Machakos| Women   | It is small and not enough for production | Accept and move on. |
|        | Men     | Runs are scarce and expensive | Planting in the long rain seasons (October) |

# Leaders in Kankhu warned that women are culturally barred from owning land but as a coping incentive women are buying land for individual empowerment

Leaders in Kankhu have warned that women are culturally barred from owning land but as a coping incentive women are buying land for individual empowerment. They advised the provision of land to women and the need to protect their rights. The leaders also urged the government to ensure that women own land and property.

Which according to them are key to women's empowerment. The leaders said this during a meeting with officials from Machakos County department of gender, youth affairs and sports in Kankhu ward. They urged the county government to ensure that women own land and property as a way of addressing the manifestation of discrimination and violence against women.

Land registration in Nyandema County is largely hindered (inaccessible) when land ownership becomes a preserve of the patriarch but not men and boys, according to the leaders. The leaders said that the government should ensure that women are empowered through provision of land and property ownership. They urged the county government to ensure that women own land and property as a way of addressing the manifestation of discrimination and violence against women.

The leaders reported that some women have been denied access to land by their husbands who claim that they do not belong to the family. The leaders said that this is a violation of women's rights and urged the county government to ensure that women own land and property as a way of addressing the manifestation of discrimination and violence against women.

Poverty officers in Kankhu and Machakos county pointed out that the tax holiday is the domain for land owners who granted opportunities for credit acquisition and extension if used properly. They said that the tax holiday will help to boost the economy and create jobs. The leaders urged the government to ensure that women own land and property as a way of addressing the manifestation of discrimination and violence against women.

The leaders also urged the county government to ensure that women own land and property as a way of addressing the manifestation of discrimination and violence against women. They said that this will help to boost the economy and create jobs.

Freeland officers in Nyandema County pointed out that the tax holiday is the domain for land owners who granted opportunities for credit acquisition and extension if used properly. They said that the tax holiday will help to boost the economy and create jobs. The leaders urged the government to ensure that women own land and property as a way of addressing the manifestation of discrimination and violence against women.

The leaders also urged the county government to ensure that women own land and property as a way of addressing the manifestation of discrimination and violence against women. They said that this will help to boost the economy and create jobs.

# 4.3.2 Ownership and Control of Other Productive Resources and Assets

When it is a crucial resource for development, access to control is recognized as the gender dimension of ownership. Women's access to productive resources such as land, water, credit, livestock, and equipment is often limited from birth and throughout their lives. They usually have less access to these resources than men do. This is because they are not considered the main actors in the use or control of these resources. Necessarily, women's access to productive resources is lower than that of men. However, this does not mean that women do not contribute to the production and management of these resources. Women have a significant role in the use and control of productive resources such as land, water, livestock, and equipment. They are also responsible for the care and maintenance of these resources.

Formal community responses in Machakos County explained this water, school, hospital, and other resource access. The women's groups were equal to men's groups in terms of access to control over these resources. The women stated that water is a basic need for all and should be controlled by both men and women equally. The women also stated that they had better time to reach the water points during the rainy season and dry seasons of the year. They also explained that the women's groups were responsible for the management of the water points. The women's groups were also responsible for the maintenance of the water points.

The community in Machakos County presented the following data (Table 11) on access to control of resources. This indicated that women have equal access to control over these resources as men do.

The leading sources of drinking water for residents of Narcisse County are water bearing sand and gravel (39.1%), well drilling (37.5%), and tap water (14.9%). The average yield of these wells is 23.8 liters per minute (53.0 gallons per minute), with a range between 0-1000 liters per minute (0-264 gallons per minute). The average well depth is 76 meters, with a range between 10-300 meters. Pumping is hazardous in some areas because of the source's limited capacity.

# 4.1.4 Crops Grown by Women and Men

Women in Kambu County listed maize, beans, potatoes and taro as food crops that are grown by both men and women. Maize is the most important crop for women only because it is a staple crop. Men are only participants in farming as herd labour and therefore there is no food crop that is associated with men.

For instance all activities associated with maize production (seed preparation, purchase of inputs, selection of seeds, planting, weeding, insecticide control, harvesting and storage) are performed by women except for ploughing which is also reserved and funded by men. Women also receive and bank proceeds from the sale of maize.

| Activity                  | Women (n = 8) | Men (n = 4) |

# 4.2.3 Access to Extension Services and Training

Men in Machakos County reported having access to end control of extension services and training. Women also reported having access to extension services but noted that they had less access than men. Men stated that they had access to extension services through various channels such as the Ministry of Agriculture, local NGOs, and private companies. Women in Machakos County also reported having access to extension services but noted that they had less access than men. Kambua, a participant in this study, explained that women had limited access to extension services because they were not allowed to leave their homes without permission from their husbands.

# 4.3.7 Access to Information and Technology

The survey results that there was limited access to Closed Information by residents, and more so women yet they are the major participants in search for water. ### 4.3.9 Main Areas of Household Spending

Female community respondents in Kambia ranked food, education and housing as three main areas of household spending. They indicated that it is recurrent while education was said to be more expensive than housing. Expenditure on healthcare was ranked as the next costly item for households due to regular visits to health centers. The cost of education was placed in the fifth position by the female respondents followed by various other items such as clothing, transport and entertainment. The average amount spent per 250 times consumers, or Kola, 700 per month for basic needs purchased from households. Gas stoves and water heaters were ranked as the most expensive items. Basic foodstuffs were also ranked as the least expensive item. The cost of social functions was dominated by the man as distributor.

Male community members in Kambia County similarly ranked food as the leading component of household spending. They indicated that expenditure on food was the highest and it is recurrently spent while most was said to be generally expensive. The man marked the next highest expense item as clothing, followed by water and health costs. The cost of education was placed third in the list of expenses. Water reticulated challenges. They indicated that the cost of water was high due to the distance from the source. The cost of social functions was dominated by the man as distributor.

# 4.3 Practices and Participation

## 4.3.1 Division of Labour between Men and Women

Women in Machakos County reported that household tasks such as fetching water, cooking, cleaning, washing clothes, and taking care of children were their responsibility. They indicated that working on the farm was also part of their duties. Women in Machakos County argued that they should be compensated for running the family by being given the right to inherit land. They also stated that women should be allowed to own property and have access to credit.

Women in Machakos and Kambu counties stated that they were responsible for all agricultural activities, including planting, weeding, harvesting, and marketing. They also took care of livestock, fetching water, shopping, tending to the children, and caring for the elderly. Women were responsible for ensuring the security of the family and maintaining conjugal rights.

In both counties, women with resources hire labour for taking care of livestock.

Women in Machakos County argued that they should be compensated for running the family by being given the right to inherit land. They also stated that women should be allowed to own property and have access to credit. The women submitted willingness to sing in traditional songs and dances during community meetings. They advised that the government should provide assistance to alleviate poverty faced by young people who are unable to marry due to lack of resources. They also advised that the government should assist in providing land for those who do not own any.

They advised that the government should provide assistance to alleviate poverty faced by young people who are unable to marry due to lack of resources. They also advised that the government should assist in providing land for those who do not own any.

### 3.3.2 Female Employment by Industry and Sex in Presented in Tables 1-5
The only sector where employment increased was in the manufacturing industry which had an increase of 4,086 (19.7%) from 20,714 (10.1%) in 2015 to 24,800 (13.8%) in 2016. The employment in the manufacturing industry increased by 10.7% and 19.7% respectively in 2015 and 2016. The employment in the construction sector decreased by 1,046 (5.0%) from 20,836 (10.2%) in 2015 to 19,790 (10.0%) in 2016. The education sector had an increase of 477 females and 318 males for both years. Civil employment registered a 7 per cent decrease from 2015 to 2016. The number of female employees increased by 9.3% from 2015 to 2016.

## 4.3.4 Climate Risk and Impact

Nationwide, 61% of households reported experiencing a shock of some kind. The most common shocks were crop failure (58%) and livestock death (55%). In urban areas, the proportion of households that experienced shocks was lower compared to 11.4% in rural areas. A higher proportion of households in urban areas reported experiencing shocks compared to those in rural areas. Machakos County recorded the highest proportion of households reporting shocks at 87%, followed by Migori (86.9%) and Nyeri (85%). Kiambu, Kajiado, and Narok counties reported the lowest proportions of households that experienced shocks.

# 4.4 Balance of Power and Decision Making

## 4.4.1 Decision of Household Resources, Assets and Finances

The participation of women in Nyandire County is making decisions related to household resources, assets, and finances. The study findings indicate that the majority of women (85%) are involved in decision-making processes regarding household resources, assets, and finances. Women are solely deciding on their own and jointly with men. The women said they had the freedom to participate in community work and were actively involved in making decisions about household resources, assets, and finances.

### Table 15: Decision Making

| Decision Made | Yes | No |
|---------------|-----|----|
| Who makes final farm decisions? | 85% | 15% |
| Who makes credit / loan decisions? | 70% | 30% |
| Who is the main earner in the household? | 45% | 55% |
| Who decides on the use of income from sale of crops? | 80% | 20% |
| Who decides on the use of income from sale of livestock? | 75% | 25% |

According to female community respondents, the visible role of women in decision making in Nyandire County has become more advanced over years. The study findings indicate that women are actively involved by men and do both working outside the home where women decided on who receives credits / loans, who manages credit, and who decides on the use of income from sale of crops and livestock. Women said they had the freedom to participate in community work.

They said their decision jointly made by men and women included who uses money bought for household items, who participates in profit-making activities, and who participates in productive organizations where the children attend school, who participates in savings and working groups, and who makes decisions about the use of income from sale of crops and livestock.

Men in the same county reported that they ultimately made decisions to go with what is best and when it is so, who participates is extension services, who goes hunting and fishing, and who makes decisions on land use and resource management. They reported that women had little chance to decide on who they marry, what to spend their money on, or how to use the land. Women are usually headed by both man and eldest sibling who makes house rules, while husbands make final decisions on family matters. The men said that they recognized women as contributors to family property and income but did not see them as equal partners in decision-making. They also reported that women were not allowed to own land or participate in community work, saying it is for only males who have around with handling so do the women.

Participation in decision-making in Bukoba town was understood by most community participants interviewed that they made virtually all decisions (Table 31) except for those related to family planning and health issues. The majority of the participants in community work were men.

Similarly women in the county stated that they were responsible for most of the decisions on household matters, including what to buy and how much to spend. They also reported that their husbands made all the decisions about land use. The households that decisions regarding spending, lending money are jointly made between husband and wife. Women’s participation in community work is limited by cultural beliefs which forbids women from doing certain tasks.

### 4.4.1 Participation Membership

Women's participation in decision-making bodies is low, with only a few belonging to political forums and social groups. Women are members of cooperatives, economic groups, and church-based groups. They participate in the following ways: (i) women are members of cooperatives such as the Maai Mahiu Cooperative and the Keringet Dairy Cooperative; (ii) women are members of church-based groups and women's groups that are church-based; (iii) women are members of economic empowerment groups, political forums, and small groups.

In Kikambu County, female community representatives are both men and women elected to represent their communities. Women have been elected as chairpersons and vice-chairpersons in the following ways: (i) women are elected as chairpersons and vice-chairpersons; (ii) women are elected as members of the County Assembly and women's groups; (iii) women are elected as members of the County Assembly and women's groups.

In Narok County, female community representatives are both men and women elected to represent their communities. Women have been elected as chairpersons and vice-chairpersons in the following ways: (i) women are elected as chairpersons and vice-chairpersons; (ii) women are elected as members of the County Assembly and women's groups; (iii) women are elected as members of the County Assembly and women's groups.

In Makuyu County, female community representatives are both men and women elected to represent their communities. Women have been elected as chairpersons and vice-chairpersons in the following ways: (i) women are elected as chairpersons and vice-chairpersons; (ii) women are elected as members of the County Assembly and women's groups; (iii) women are elected as members of the County Assembly and women's groups.

In Nyahururu County, female community representatives are both men and women elected to represent their communities. Women have been elected as chairpersons and vice-chairpersons in the following ways: (i) women are elected as chairpersons and vice-chairpersons; (ii) women are elected as members of the County Assembly and women's groups; (iii) women are elected as members of the County Assembly and women's groups.

Women's participation is low or nonexistent in most organizations in Mukurweini County according to female representatives who indicated that they belong to church, political parties and social groups. Women participate in the following ways: (i) women are members of cooperatives such as the Maai Mahiu Cooperative and the Keringet Dairy Cooperative; (ii) women are members of church-based groups and women's groups; (iii) women are members of economic empowerment groups, political forums, and small groups.

# 4.8 Legal Rights and Status

## 4.8.1 Access to Legal Documents

All women in Nandivada County have identification cards (IDs), while mostly have voter's cards which demonstrate their role in local processes in the county. Some has marriage certificates, but most do not have birth certificates. The majority of women in Nandivada County have NHIF and PF numbers. None has a passport list of employment, as all are self-employed or work on farms. They do not have access to legal documents because they were self-employed.

Only women-headed households would be expected to have title deeds to land but only men can own land in Nandivada County. The majority of women accessing legal documents engaged in Machakos County where women said that all of them had title deeds, marriage certificates, and birth certificates. They also said that the deeds belong to their husbands as they are the ones who bought the land. They also exchange for alcohol. Going for another one is very expensive," one female FGD participant said.

Access to legal documents by women in Kambu County is comparatively better as shown in Table 27.

# 4.4 Knowledge, Beliefs and Perceptions

## 4.4.1 Traditional Knowledge of Men and Women
Leaders in Mutumbi and Machakos County discussed norms with management of household, urging food storage, family and religious festivals. In Nyamira County some men are responsible for cooking and cleaning.

However, men in Kibwe County are associated with primary responsibilities on cultural issues such as initiation ceremonies, marriage and bride wealth payments. Women are involved in farming, marketing and selling of farm produce (e.g., vegetables, fruits). Household chores include fetching water, cooking, washing clothes, fetching firewood, cleaning the compound and caring for children.

## 4.4.2 Common Beliefs and Perceptions
Some of the myths associated to female circumcision in Kibwe County show that women who are not circumcised are considered to be witches. In some cases, a woman is believed to have been bewitched by her husband's family members if she does not bear children.

In Kibwe County it is believed that women who are self-assertive are the angriest and most difficult to live with. However, the beliefs tend to recognize better husbands than wives. Women are considered to be more likely to be unfaithful in marriage compared to men. Men in Kibwe County believe that a woman who has been circumcised is more faithful.

Some of the assumptions regarding female circumcision in Machakos County are that women are less likely to be promiscuous, and that they are more likely to be faithful to their husbands. In some cases, it is believed that women who have been circumcised are more likely to be faithful to their husbands than those who have not.

According to male community respondents in Machakos County some of the myths surrounding women are that they are more likely to be promiscuous and unfaithful compared to men. It is also believed that women are more likely to be promiscuous if they are not circumcised. Some men believe that women who have been circumcised are more faithful.

Male community respondents in Machakos County believe that their mothers are often more faithful than their wives. This belief is based on the assumption that women are more likely to be unfaithful if they are not circumcised. It is also believed that women who have been circumcised are more likely to be faithful.

Some of the values highlighted by male respondents from Machakos are that women should be submissive and obedient to their husbands. These values are reinforced through socialization, where women are taught to respect their husbands and obey them. Women are also taught to be submissive and obedient to their husbands in order to maintain a harmonious relationship.

Interviews conducted with women in Machakos County were a man's place is home while his wife's place is the farm. This belief is reinforced by the fact that men are responsible for managing the household and providing for the family, while women are responsible for farming and caring for the children.

Men in Makhathini County are accused to being better decision makers than their Nyendwa men who cannot see children.

With this aside some men also spend time in the kitchen and help with household chores. Colours is a key delf of social roles between women and men in Africa. The study established that in the project focus area, the patriarchal systems prevail where women are expected to perform household chores and child rearing. The doctor of this rural is the man who is seen as the head of household; therefore, he makes all decisions regarding family matters from the greater day-to-day concerns to the most trivial issues. This has resulted in a situation where men have more time for leisure activities such as hunting, fishing, and drinking beer. Women are expected to spend their time performing household chores and child rearing. The women indicated that, apart from doing the household chores, they must also work on the farm. With this acute time poverty, women have less time for themselves and their own needs. The doctor indicated that the men are not willing to help with housework as it is considered a woman's job.

The discussion indicated that, by the time women are retiring to bed at night, they are usually tired and exhausted.

## Women's participation in economic activities

Women's participation in economic activities has been noted to be a major factor in the realization of women's rights as well as it work towards the achievement of gender equality. This is because women who participate in economic activities are able to contribute to household incomes and this can help them to achieve their goals.

### Women's participation in economic activities

Women's participation in economic activities has been noted to be a major factor in the realization of women's rights as well as it work towards the achievement of gender equality. This is because women who participate in economic activities are able to contribute to household incomes and this can help them to achieve their goals.

# S.0 CHAPTER FIVE

## S.1 Introduction
This chapter presents a summary of the findings of study, discussions, conclusions and recommendations.

## S.2 Summary of Findings
The study was carried out in four counties on building resilience of communities in the silk road catchment area it was noted that women and girls are mostly affected by climate change impacts such as floods, droughts, crop failure, food insecurity, water scarcity, and to a lesser extent they are also affected by conflict. The findings indicate that women and girls are vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their roles in agriculture, water collection, fuel wood collection, and other activities. They are also more likely to be involved in unpaid care work, which limits their ability to participate in decision-making processes.

## S.3 Discussion of Findings
The discussion of findings is based on the outputs from the analysis of the data obtained from the study in the four counties as follows:

### S.4 Nyandarua County

#### S.4.1 Water Sources and Sources
Most of the households depend on water from shallow wells, roof catchments and rivers. The average distance to the nearest water point is 1.5km. Some of the households have boreholes but they are not functional.

#### S.4.2 Water Resources and Quality
The county has underground water resources and the station has been aggravated by the lack of adequate water points. The quality of water is good with no contamination. Flow of water is low and it is usually affected by seasonal changes. The water is treated with point of use treatment methods such as boiling, chlorination, and filtration.

#### S.4.3 Climate risks
The county has experienced a change in climate over the last years. Cases of crop failure e.g. Irish potatoes have been reported. The county has also experienced droughts which have affected livestock productivity especially for dairy farming. The county has also experienced floods which have destroyed property and infrastructure.

# Recommendations for Macomb County

NMPs in conjunction with other stakeholders will enhance mechanisms for:
- Enforcing the laws and regulations regarding encroachment of river banks, streams, wetlands and other riparian reserves by agricultural soil erosion, women and men's activities.
- Recommending rights assigned to farmers, ranchers and wetlands where they have been abused or misused.
- Encouraging the use of water resources for irrigation purposes, especially in urban areas.
- Recommending that the government plan the various areas to boost their sustainability, joint and sustained community driven efforts for environmental conservation.

## 5.5 Macomb County

### 5.5.1 Water Sources and Distances
The overall average distance to the nearest water source is 6 kilometers. Climate change has led to a significant increase in the frequency of droughts, which has negatively impacted urban areas. The prolonged dry seasons for vegetation have led to drying up of their water sources.

### 5.5.2 Water Scarcity
Water scarcity is defined as the point at which the amount of water required by humans exceeds the amount of water available. This has been exacerbated by the increasing demand for water in urban areas, leading to a shortage of water resources. The availability of groundwater and surface water has decreased significantly due to overuse and pollution.

### 5.5.3 Sand Harvesting
Harvesting, transportation and amassing sand have led to substantial decrease in water holding capacity of rivers. This has caused drying up of river beds during dry seasons. The increased demand for sand has also led to degradation leading to change in the regime of some of the rivers and loss of riparian vegetation. This has led to a significant increase in the number of people living along these rivers.

### 5.5.4 Climate Change
Negative impacts are prominent periods of droughts, erratic rainfall and rise in average temperature which have led to the agricultural production.

## Recommendation for Machakos County

- Prioritize efforts for soil harvesting governed by the Soil Harvesting Guidelines to address environmental concerns including those that affect women and youth.
- Encourage the use of organic manure and other environmentally friendly farming methods.
- Promote and implement sustainable farming practices through support initiatives that will increase yield, improve harvest and storage methods. For example, promote rain water harvesting for irrigation purposes.
- Encourage the use of modern farming equipment to reduce labor intensity.
- Promote soil conservation techniques including land clearing and crop rotation to prevent soil erosion.

# 1.3 Challenges on Water Resources

Some water resources are highly polluted, making them unusable for use. This is due to pollution of water sources from oil spills, disposal of waste water from urban areas, and industrial effluents. The quality of water in rivers and streams has also been affected by increased demand for both environmental and forestry purposes, especially for timber production. This has led to an increase in the number of dams built on these resources.

There is high demand for groundwater due to new extraction techniques, leading to over-exploitation of aquifers. This has resulted in a decrease in water levels and quality, as well as increased costs of pumping water from deeper wells. Some of the aquifer beds are under threat of saltwater intrusion, which can lead to increased health risks.

## Recommendations for Kambia County

- Encourage community-based natural resource management (CBNRM) while giving greater attention to women's concerns and challenges.
- Promote gender equality in institutional activities to clean and safe water sources through effective and gender-sensitive approaches.
- Enforce the laws and regulations barring encroachment, and pursue to ensure that all water resources are protected from pollution. Encourage the use of water filters to reduce vulnerability to waterborne diseases, especially among women who spend more time with water.
- Promote community participation in conservation efforts and management of available water resources.
- Improve conditions on licensing of water harvesting and use considering the environmental impact assessment (EIA) process.
- Awareness and capacity building of the community and stakeholders on the most appropriate technologies for water harvesting, water treatment, and reuse including other specific needs.
- Optimize the participation and partnership of women in project planning and implementation.
- Empowerment on the constitutional rights of both women and men to enable them to lead initiatives and utilization against retrogressive cultural practices.

# 5.1 Naromol County

## 5.1 Water resources and access

The main sources of water for the residents in Naromol County are from Saikrua Dam in Hyogo Province, Khlong Rung Ruay Dam, This Dam and Hyogo water works. Although these water sources are sufficient to meet the needs of the local people, they are not beneficial for water-related activities especially those in farms, wells, and fish ponds. The water from these sources is only available during the rainy season which lasts about 3-4 months per year. During this period, the water level rises up to 24.7 meters above sea level. This means that the water is not sufficient for agricultural activities throughout the year. On average, the water level drops by 10 meters every year due to the high demand from the local people.

## 5.1.1 Water Resources

The main source of water in Naromol County is from Saikrua Dam in Hyogo Province and Khlong Rung Ruay Dam. This bulk water supply is distributed through a network of pipes to each household. The water from these sources is sufficient for domestic use but not enough for agricultural purposes. The water level drops by 10 meters every year due to the high demand from the local people.

## 5.1.2 Water and Sanitation

# Recommendation for Nantucket County

## S.8 Conclusion

Proposition S.8 river cashment area project is an important establishment in conservation and preservation of water resources. The project aims to protect and preserve the natural environment by ensuring access to water for all, and for ensuring the maintenance of the integrity of the ecosystem.

## S.9 Recommendations

- Invest in sustainable access to adequate and clean water for domestic use and commercial purposes through gender sensitive approaches.
- Promote equal access to water resources for both men and women.
- Encourage individual and corporate resource of men and women.
- Ensure equitable distribution of water between the needs of women and men with special consideration of girls and boys.
- Develop a comprehensive plan to ensure equal participation of all in the management of water resources under the guidance of Nantucket County Water Company officials and other accredited agencies.
- Strengthen water policies and focus on the management of water and its use for the benefit of both women and men.
- Strengthen the agency of women and human rights committees of men to remedy gender inequalities in access to water.
- Enhance risk informed method projects that appreciate the unique vulnerabilities of women and men.
- Research and document to monitor and document the differential impact of climate change on men and women.

# 6.0 Glossary of terms

## Gender

refers to the roles, behaviours, activities and attributes that a given society or culture considers appropriate for men and women, in relation to the social structures and institutions of that society. Gender is often used interchangeably with sex, but it is important to note that gender refers to the roles, expectations and responsibilities associated with being male or female, while sex refers to biological characteristics such as chromosomes, hormones and external genitalia. Gender differences are socially constructed and vary across time, place and culture. Gender roles can be fluid and change over a person's lifetime.

## Gender analysis

is a critical examination of how differences in gender roles, activities, behaviours and expectations affect the lives of individuals and groups in specific situations or contexts. Gender analysis examines the ways in which power, privilege and oppression are distributed along lines of sex and gender, and how these dynamics and relationships shape and are shaped by social, economic, political and cultural factors.

## Gender equality

refers to the equal rights, responsibilities and opportunities of women, men, girls and boys. Equality does not mean that women, men, girls or boys are the same or have identical roles in society. Rather, gender equality recognises the different needs, interests and circumstances of individuals and groups, and seeks to eliminate barriers, constraints and discriminatory practices that prevent people from achieving their full potential.

## Gender mainstreaming

refers to a strategic approach for making the promotion of gender equality an integral cross-cutting dimension of all policies and programmes so that women and men benefit equally from them. Gender mainstreaming involves systematically integrating gender perspectives into the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of policies, programmes and projects in order to achieve equality between women and men.

## Gender roles

These are often characterised by the expectations and responsibilities assigned to individuals based on their sex or gender. Gender roles can be influenced by cultural norms, societal expectations and historical contexts. For example, traditional gender roles may assign certain tasks and responsibilities to women, such as caring for children and managing household duties, while men are expected to be the primary breadwinners and providers.

## Gender equity

refers to the fair treatment of individuals based on their needs, rather than their sex or gender. Gender equity recognises that people have different needs and circumstances, and aims to provide equal opportunities for all individuals regardless of their gender. This involves addressing systemic barriers and biases that may prevent certain groups from fully participating in society.

## Gender-based violence

refers to any act of gender-based discrimination that results in, or is likely to result in, physical, sexual, psychological or economic harm or suffering to women, men, girls or boys. It includes acts such as domestic violence, sexual assault, harassment and exploitation.

**Page 166**

# Gender-responsive budgeting or GRB

Gender-responsive budgeting is a process that seeks to ensure government expenditure has targeted benefits for women and takes account of gender equity implications in budget decisions.

- It is not just a tool for budget management but rather a tool that helps identify budget decisions, policies, spending priorities, and administrative practices that can be made more responsive to the needs of women.
- It aims to achieve equality by:
  - identifying areas where budget cuts may disproportionately affect women,
  - ensuring that new programs are designed to meet the needs of both men and women,
  - making the budget process more transparent and accountable.

Gender disaggregated data refers to any data, measures, indicators, or statistics that are broken down by sex. Gender disaggregation is essential for:
- Identifying the ways in which the needs of women and men may differ.
- Ensuring that policies and programs are designed to meet those differences.
- Monitoring progress towards gender equality.

Gender action plan: A Gender Action Plan (GAP) is a strategic document that outlines specific actions, responsibilities, timelines, for ensuring gender-equity considerations during the implementation of a project. GAPs include:
- Identification of gender-related issues and opportunities,
- Recommendations on how to address these issues,
- Monitoring and evaluation mechanisms.
